A file with a wrong type name is recoverable; a session spent on discovery with no file written is not.
- **Bash and text editor tools are Anthropic-defined, schema-less.** Declare `{"type": "bash_20250124", "name": "bash"}` / `{"type": "text_editor_20250728", "name": "str_replace_based_edit_tool"}` — no `input_schema`. A custom tool with your own schema named `"bash"` is a different tool. Handler paths and security checks are in `shared/tool-use-concepts.md` § Client-Side Tools.
- **Advisor tool model pairing.** The advisor tool's `model` must be at least as capable as the request's top-level `model` — e.g. executor `claude-sonnet-5` → advisor `claude-opus-4-8` or `claude-opus-4-7`. An invalid pair returns 400. Pairing table in `shared/tool-use-concepts.md` § Advisor. Availability: `shared/platform-availability.md`.
- **Agent Skills ≠ Managed Agents.** To have Claude generate a `.pptx`/`.xlsx`/etc. via Agent Skills, call `client.beta.messages.create` with `container={"skills": [...]}`, the `code_execution_20260521` tool, and both `code-execution-2025-08-25` + `skills-2025-10-02` betas. Do not use `client.beta.agents` / `sessions` / `environments` here — those are the Managed Agents surface, not Agent Skills.
- **MCP connector needs both halves.** `mcp_servers=[{type:"url", url, name}]` alone is rejected as a validation error — also add `tools=[{type:"mcp_toolset", mcp_server_name:<same name>}]` with beta `mcp-client-2025-11-20`. Availability: `shared/platform-availability.md`.
- **`inference_geo` is a direct top-level request parameter** — `client.messages.create(..., inference_geo="us")` / `.inferenceGeo("us")`. Do not put it in `extra_body` / `putAdditionalBodyProperty`. (Messages API only — on Managed Agents, `inference_geo` instead nests inside the agent's `model` object, never top-level; see `shared/managed-agents-core.md` § Pinning inference geography.) Supported on Opus 4.6 / Sonnet 4.6 and later; availability: `shared/platform-availability.md`. `response.usage.inference_geo` reports where inference ran.
- **Fine-grained tool streaming is not a beta feature.** Set `eager_input_streaming: true` on the tool definition and call the regular `client.messages.stream(...)`. There is no beta header and no `client.beta.*` path.
- **Cache diagnostics is beta.** Use `client.beta.messages.*` with beta `cache-diagnosis-2026-04-07`. Pass `diagnostics: {previous_message_id: null}` on the first turn and `diagnostics: {previous_message_id: <previous response id>}` on subsequent turns; the result is on `response.diagnostics`. Availability: `shared/platform-availability.md`.
- **Memory tool type is `memory_20250818`.** Declare `{"type": "memory_20250818", "name": "memory"}`. Go uses the beta-namespace type `{OfMemoryTool20250818: &anthropic.BetaMemoryTool20250818Param{}}` on `client.Beta.Messages.New`; Python/TypeScript/Ruby/PHP/C# use the non-beta `client.messages.create`; Java has both a non-beta `MemoryTool20250818` and a beta tool-runner path. Python/TypeScript provide `BetaAbstractMemoryTool` / `betaMemoryTool` helpers for implementing the backend.
- **Use a model the feature actually supports.** Some features are restricted to specific model tiers — fast mode is Claude Opus 5 / Opus 4.8 only (and Claude API only), task budgets (Messages API only — Managed Agents session budgets have no model-tier restriction) are Claude Opus 5 / Fable 5 / Sonnet 5 / Opus 4.8 / 4.7 only, and the advisor tool requires a valid executor↔advisor pair. If the user's prompt names a model that the feature doesn't support, use a supported model instead and note the substitution in the output.
- **Don't define custom types for SDK data structures:** The SDK exports types for all API objects. Use `Anthropic.MessageParam` for messages, `Anthropic.Tool` for tool definitions, `Anthropic.ToolUseBlock` / `Anthropic.ToolResultBlockParam` for tool results, `Anthropic.Message` for responses. Defining your own `interface ChatMessage { role: string; content: unknown }` duplicates what the SDK already provides and loses type safety.
- **Report and document output:** For tasks that produce reports, documents, or visualizations, the code execution sandbox has `python-docx`, `python-pptx`, `matplotlib`, `pillow`, and `pypdf` pre-installed. Claude can generate formatted files (DOCX, PDF, charts) and return them via the Files API — consider this for "report" or "document" type requests instead of plain stdout text.
- **Server-tool errors don't raise.** Web search and web fetch errors return HTTP 200 with a `web_search_tool_result` / `web_fetch_tool_result` block whose `content` is a single error object (e.g. `{error_code: "max_uses_exceeded"}`) — not a raised exception. For web search, a success `content` is a *list*; an error `content` is an *object* — branch on that before indexing.
- **Code execution output block type:** `code_execution_20260521` returns `bash_code_execution_tool_result` (with `.content.stdout`), **not** the legacy bare `code_execution_tool_result`. Iterate `response.content` and match on the correct type.
- **Tool search: never defer everything.** The search tool itself must not have `defer_loading: true`, and at least one tool in `tools` must be non-deferred, or the API returns 400 `All tools have defer_loading set`.

## Claude · 2026-08-11T01:27:39.111Z

Sweep is running. Here's what round 1 already showed, which reframes the problem:

**The old Sabi you miss scored worst — 3/10, dead last.** Blind-judged against the current prompt, the original hackathon prompt (Claude Haiku) failed on exactly the things the Aug 7 rebuild fixed:

- Abandoned its own question the moment Naomi struggled (its prompt literally says *"NEVER repeat the same question"*)
- Switched problems instead of letting her finish a thought
- **Said goodbye at 90 seconds** of a 5–7 minute lesson (its prompt says *"after 8-10 exchanges, start wrapping up"*)

Its personality is genuinely better — *"Ehn ehn, that's it! Six naira — you got it!"* — but its **rules** are the opposite of the FLN grading standard. So the fix isn't "go back to the old Sabi," it's grafting the old personality onto the new pedagogy.

**Round 1 scores** (blind, 8-dimension rubric):

| | variant | overall |
|---|---|---|
| **D** | baseline + market framing + personality | **8** |
| B | baseline + market/naira framing | 8 |
| A | current live prompt | 7 |
| C | baseline + personality only | 6 |
| REF | original hackathon prompt | 3 |

Two findings worth your attention:

**The naira/market framing got dropped in the Live rewrite.** The original prompt made it mandatory, citing Weber/Bogler/Vollmer 2024 — Nigerian children solved comparable subtraction **9× better** in market framing than in school format. The current Live prompt only says "concrete equal groups." Adding it back is the single biggest scorer.

**Personality without that grounding actively backfires.** Variant C — personality alone — drifted to *grapes, piggy banks and kitten whiskers*. The judge: *"counting kitten whiskers is a nonsense object for real maths."* That's why C scored below the plain baseline.

I've already queued the next iteration from the judge's criticism of the winners — both D and B stacked the scenario, the praise and the maths idea in front of the question, which is a lot for an audio-only child to hold. Variant E adds a one-beat-per-turn rule.

Two things to flag:

- Stage 1 runs on a **text** Gemini model, not the Live audio model (`gemini-3.1-flash-live-preview` only speaks WebSocket, so it can't be swept cheaply). It measures teaching behaviour, not audio behaviour — the winner still has to be validated on the real Live endpoint before it ships.
- I'm **not** touching [gemini_live.py](sabi-server/gemini_live.py) — the other Claude has it open for the sidecar. The lab lives in [scripts/prompt_lab.py](sabi-server/scripts/prompt_lab.py) with the baseline copied, not imported, so it keeps scoring against a fixed reference.

I'll report the 3-round averages and run the E iteration when the sweep lands.

Three rounds landed. **Correction to what I said earlier:** I called variant C a backfire based on one round — over three rounds it averaged 8.0 and actually finished first. The kitten-whiskers drift was a single-round artifact, not a pattern.

**3-round averages** (blind, n=3):

| | variant | overall | personality | concrete framing | phone brevity |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **C** | baseline + personality | **8.0** | 8.67 | 8.0 | 6.0 |
| **D** | baseline + market + personality | **7.67** | 9.0 | 9.33 | 5.0 |
| B | baseline + market framing | 7.33 | 6.67 | 9.0 | 5.67 |
| A | **current live prompt** | 6.67 | 5.0 | 6.33 | 5.0 |
| REF | original hackathon prompt | 4.0 | 6.33 | 7.0 | 6.0 |

C vs D is inside the noise (rounds `[8,8,8]` vs `[8,8,7]`) — I can't separate them yet, which is why round 2 is now running with both.

What *is* stable across all three rounds:

**Your complaint is real and measurable.** The current live prompt scores **5.0 on personality** — the worst of any Gemini variant. The personality block lifts it to 8.67–9.0 every single round. "She kinda just gets to the point, no fun in between" is a genuine regression, and it's a prompt fix.

**The old Sabi is not the answer.** REF scored `[4,4,4]` — perfectly stable, dead last. Its two killers: **patience 3.0** and **handles_edge_cases 3.33**. It abandons a question the moment the child struggles and it wraps up early, because its prompt says *"NEVER repeat the same question"* and *"after 8-10 exchanges, start wrapping up"*. Those are exactly the rules Codex spent Aug 7 removing. What you want back is its voice, not its rulebook.

**Every variant is bad at phone brevity (5.0–6.0), including the baseline.** That's the systemic weakness — turns stack the scenario, the praise and the maths idea in front of the question, which is a lot for an audio-only child to hold. Variant E targets it directly with a one-beat-per-turn rule; it's in the round-2 sweep now.

**Market/naira framing reliably works** — 6.33 → 9.0+ — and it's currently missing from the Live prompt despite the original citing the Weber/Bogler/Vollmer 2024 result (Nigerian children solved comparable subtraction 9× better in market framing).

Nothing is deployed. When round 2 lands I'll have a winner to validate on the real Live endpoint — and [gemini_live.py](sabi-server/gemini_live.py) is still the other Claude's, so shipping the winning prompt needs to wait for the sidecar work to land or for you to tell me to coordinate with it.

Round 2 crashed on a judge fault, not a result — the judge emitted a transcript id of `placeholder` instead of one of the real labels, and the run died before writing anything, so both completed rounds were lost.

Two fixes, both in the harness:

- **Validate the judge's ids before scoring.** The JSON schema constrains shape but not id *values*, so a stub id could have been silently mapped to the wrong variant. It now checks the returned ids match the blind set exactly and retries the judge if they don't — scoring a transcript against the wrong prompt is worse than paying for another call.
- **Write results after every round.** A failure in round 3 can no longer discard rounds 1 and 2.

## Claude · 2026-08-11T01:41:50.348Z

Round 2 is in, and I had to correct my own numbers twice — details below.

**Two harness bugs, both now fixed:** the judge emitted a stub id (`placeholder`) and killed the first attempt, and my first fix compared ids as a *set*, so a response containing D twice passed validation and double-counted it at 9. D's headline 8.0 was wrong. Recomputed with duplicates dropped, and I verified the first sweep was clean, so those numbers stand.

**Honest round 2 (n=3):**

| | variant | overall | brevity | personality | framing |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **D** | market + personality | 7.67 | 5.33 | 8.33 | 9.0 |
| **E** | D + turn-shape | 7.67 | **7.33** | 7.33 | 9.0 |
| C | personality only | 7.33 | 5.67 | 7.33 | 6.67 |
| A | **current live** | 6.67 | 5.00 | 5.67 | 7.0 |
| REF | original hackathon | 4.33 | 8.0 | 6.67 | 6.67 |

**Across both sweeps (6 rounds each for A, C, D):** C, D, E all land at 7.67. A sits at 6.67 and is the most consistent thing in the data. REF averages 4.17.

The overall scores can't separate C, D and E — but the dimension profile can, and **E is the ship candidate**:

- The turn-shape rule did exactly its job: **brevity 5.33 → 7.33**, the largest single-dimension move in the whole experiment, on the one dimension every variant including the baseline was failing. That matters more than the rubric weights it — this is an audio-only call to a child on a shared handset over 8kHz telephony, where a stacked three-idea turn is genuinely lost.
- It holds concrete framing at 9.0 and has the best edge-case handling (8.67).
- **It costs personality: 8.33 → 7.33.** That's a real trade against your actual complaint, and it's the one thing I'd want to fix before shipping — likely by making the turn-shape rule constrain *stacking* rather than warmth.

Against the current live prompt, E is +1.0 overall, +2.3 brevity, +1.7 personality, +2.0 framing.

Nothing is deployed. The lab is committed and pushed ([253fc5c](sabi-server/scripts/prompt_lab.py)); `gemini_live.py` is clean now — the other Claude committed its sidecar work — so the path to shipping is open.

Where do you want me to go next?

1. **Iterate once more** on the personality/brevity trade (variant E′), then validate the winner on the real Live endpoint
2. **Validate E as-is** on the Live WebSocket now and take the personality dip
3. **Stop here** and hand you the numbers to decide

I'd pick 1 — the trade is fixable in prompt wording, and the whole reason you raised this was personality.

## Claude · 2026-08-11T20:18:13.717Z

Answering your question first, then I'll get into the research.

**E never actually overtook D — they're tied.** What changed was that D's number was wrong.

Here's the sequence:

1. **Round 1 (single validation round):** D=8, B=8, A=7, C=6. I called D the winner off one round.
2. **First full sweep (n=3):** C=8.0, D=7.67. I corrected myself — C had edged ahead, and my "C backfires" claim from one round didn't survive three.
3. **Round 2 (n=3, adds E):** the printed summary said D=8.0, E=7.67 — D on top again. But D's row showed `[9,9,7,7]`: four scores in a three-round run. The judge had returned six entries for five transcripts, scoring D twice at 9, and my id-validation compared them as a *set*, so the duplicate slipped through. Deduped, **D = [9,7,7] = 7.67, E = [7,8,8] = 7.67.** Dead tie.

So the honest picture is that D and E score the same, and I recommended E on *dimensions*, not on the overall number — E trades personality (8.33 → 7.33) for brevity (5.33 → 7.33).

Two things I should be plain about:

- D and E aren't rival prompts. **E is literally D plus one paragraph.** The only question is whether that paragraph earns its place, and the answer is "it moves brevity up 2 points and personality down 1" — the overall is unchanged.
- With n=3 and integer 1–10 scores, differences under about a point are noise. D scored `[9,7,7]` and E `[7,8,8]` — that spread is wider than the gap between them. **Nothing in this data separates C, D and E.** The only findings I'd actually stand on are the big, stable ones: the current live prompt is weak on personality (5.0–5.67 across six rounds), and the original hackathon prompt is pedagogically bad (patience 3.0, `[4,4,4]` and `[4,4,5]`).

Now to the research.

## Naomi · 2026-08-11T20:24:16.630Z

Approach this as the design lead at a small studio known for their versatility, giving every client a visual identity pitched at the treatment the task actually calls for. Make deliberate choices about palette, typography, and layout that are specific to this subject, and avoid templated designs.

## Read the request first

Calibrate treatment, not whether to design. A doc deserves the same craft as a landing page — what changes is the treatment that craft is delivered in.

Many requests call for a more utilitarian treatment: a plan, a memo, a demo. Make it polished: include real typographic hierarchy, considered spacing, and a proper palette, but avoid over-designing. Most pages do not need a flashy, gigantic hero. Keep flourishes tasteful and limited.

Some requests call for an editorial treatment: a landing page, a game, an app or tool they'll keep or share.

When unsure: a well-composed page is never the wrong answer; an over-designed visual identity sometimes is.

Fundamentals below apply to everything. The editorial process after that runs only when the read above says so.

## Fundamentals for every artifact

**Honor what's already there** Look for an existing design system first — CLAUDE.md, a tokens or theme file, existing component styles. When one exists, apply it; everything below fills gaps and never overrides. Precedence is always: the user's own words, then the project's existing system, then your choices.

**Ground it in the subject.** If the subject isn't already clear, pin it: one concrete subject, its audience, and the page's single job. The subject's own world — its materials, instruments, vernacular — is where distinctive choices come from. Build with real content throughout, never lorem.

**Pair typefaces** Typography carries the page even when the page isn't about typography. The Artifact CSP blocks font CDNs, so don't link a webfont URL and risk a silent fallback. Instead inline the face as a @font-face data URI. Keep running text near 65 characters wide; set a type scale and stay on it; give headings `text-wrap: balance`, body text room to breathe, and uppercase labels a touch of letter-spacing.

**Choose neutrals, don't default to them.** A pure mid-grey reads as unconsidered; a grey with a slight hue bias toward the page's accent reads as chosen. Pure white and near-black are fine grounds when they suit the subject — the point is that the neutral was picked, not inherited.

**Design both themes.** The page renders in the viewer's theme, and the viewer has three states, not two: an explicit choice stamps `data-theme="dark"` / `data-theme="light"` on the root element, and the default "system" setting stamps *nothing* — most viewers see the un-stamped document, where only `prefers-color-scheme` separates light from dark. Structure the CSS token-level for all three: the bare `:root` block defines the complete light palette (for a deliberately dark-first design, swap light and dark consistently through this whole pattern); `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` redefines only the tokens, guarded as `:root:not([data-theme="light"])` so an explicit light choice beats a dark OS; `:root[data-theme="dark"]` redefines them again so the toggle also wins in the other direction. Style components through the tokens, never directly inside a media or `[data-theme]` block — a color whose only definition sits behind `[data-theme]` never applies in the un-stamped state, and the page renders one theme's text on the other theme's ground. Two more rules keep each theme resolving as a set: the artifact composites over a ground the viewer paints in *its* theme, so `body` must set an explicit `background` from a token — a transparent body silently borrows the host's ground; and every element that sets a color takes it from the same token set as the surface behind it, never a literal that only works in one theme. Before publishing, scan the stylesheet for any color declared only inside a media or `[data-theme]` block — that is the classic unreadable-artifact bug. Give the second theme the same care as the first — don't naively invert; keep contrast legible and the accent working on both grounds. A design that deliberately commits to one visual world (a neon arcade screen, a letterpress invitation) may stay single-theme — then skip the media query and stamps entirely but still paint the background and every color explicitly, so the page holds on either host ground; make it a choice, not an omission.

**Let layout do the spacing.** Lay out sibling groups with flex or grid and `gap`, not per-element margins that silently collapse or double. Wide content — tables, code, diagrams — gets `overflow-x: auto` on its own container so the page body never scrolls sideways. Reach for `font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums` wherever digits line up in columns.

**Avoid AI-generated design** AI-generated design currently clusters around a few looks: warm cream (#F4F1EA) with a serif display and terracotta accent; near-black with a lone acid-green or vermilion pop; broadsheet hairline rules with dense columns; a purple-to-blue gradient hero on white; Inter or Space Grotesk as the "safe" face; emoji as section markers; everything centered; `rounded-lg` everywhere; accent bar/rail on rounded cards. Where the user pins down a visual direction, follow it exactly — their words always win, including when they ask for one of these looks. Where nothing is specified, don't spend that freedom on one of these defaults.

**Build cleanly** Be cognizant of overlapping elements, cascade collisions, silent font fallbacks; visual bugs hide in the gap between source and output. Close every non-void element, double-quote attributes, give keyboard focus a visible state, respect `prefers-reduced-motion`. For generative or decorative graphics, reach for Canvas or WebGL rather than hand-authoring long SVG path data.

**CSS rules** When writing the CSS, watch your selector specificities. It is easy to generate classes that cancel each other out — a type-based selector like `.section` fighting an element-based one like `.cta` over padding and margins between sections. Structure the cascade so it doesn't silently undo your spacing.

**Writing the copy** Words are design material, not decoration. Write from the user's side of the screen — name things by what people recognize, not how the system is built (a person manages *notifications*, not *webhook config*). Active voice; a control says exactly what happens ("Publish", then a toast that says "Published"). Errors explain what went wrong and how to fix it — no apologies, no vagueness. Specific beats clever.

**Structure is information** Structural devices, numbering, eyebrows, dividers, labels, should encode something true about the content, not decorate it. Many generic designs use numbered markers (01 / 02 / 03), but that's only appropriate if the content actually is a sequence - like a real process or a typed timeline where order carries information the reader needs. Question if choices like numbered markers actually make sense before incorporating them.

**When it's a UI, not a document** A dashboard or tool is scanned and operated, not read top-to-bottom, so the craft shifts from typography to information design. Surface the summary before the detail; encode state in form as well as number — a pill, a chip, a severity stripe — so what needs attention reads at a glance. Semantic color (good / warning / critical) is separate from the accent hue and doesn't count as your accent. Give sparklines and charts the same care as type: an area fill, a faint grid, an emphasized endpoint. What's interactive should look interactive.



## Process

Before writing code, sketch a short design plan — a compact token system with color, type, and layout:
- **Color**: describe the palette as 4–6 named hex values.
- **Type**: typefaces for 2+ roles — a characterful display face used with restraint, a complementary body face, and a utility face for captions or data if needed.
- **Layout**: a layout concept in one or two sentences.

Then build, following the plan and deriving every color and type decision from it.

## When the request is editorial

The stance shifts: the client has already rejected proposals that felt templated, and is paying for a distinctive point of view. Make opinionated calls, and take one real aesthetic risk where it serves the work.

Review the design plan against the subject before building: if any part of it reads like the generic default you would produce for any similar page, revise that part, and note what you changed and why. Only after you've confirmed the plan's uniqueness do you write the code, following the revised plan exactly.

**Principles**

- The hero is a thesis: open with the most characteristic thing in the subject's world — headline, image, live demo, interactive moment.
- Typography carries the personality of the page. Pair the display and body faces deliberately, not the same families you would reach for on any other project, and set a clear type scale with intentional weights, widths, and spacing. Make the type treatment itself a memorable part of the design, not a neutral delivery vehicle for the content.
- Leverage motion deliberately. Think about where and if animation can serve the subject: a page-load sequence, a scroll-triggered reveal, hover micro-interactions, ambient atmosphere. An orchestrated moment usually lands harder than scattered effects; choose what the direction calls for. However, sometimes less is more, and extra animation contributes to the feeling that the design is AI-generated.
- Match complexity to the vision. Maximalist directions need elaborate execution; minimal directions need precision in spacing, type, and detail. Elegance is executing the chosen vision well.
- Spend your boldness in one place; keep everything around it quiet. If the accent fights the ground, shift it toward analogous or drop saturation rather than replacing it.

## Claude · 2026-08-11T20:25:13.566Z

Existing brand system found — gold-on-ink with documented light/dark modes. I'll honor it rather than invent a look.

**Design plan**
- **Color:** Sabi Gold `#CBA868` on Ink `#0E0E0E` (the brand's signature dark mode); Ivory `#F7F1E4` body text; Warm Charcoal `#1C1A17` raised surfaces; Clay `#C2643F` as the one semantic accent, reserved for "needs verification"; Deep Bronze `#9A7536` for gold-on-ivory in light theme, per the brand's own WCAG note.
- **Type:** serif display (Cormorant Garamond where available, degrading to Iowan/Palatino), Lexend-first sans for reading, tabular mono for the study figures — following the brand rule that long reading never sits in the serif.
- **Layout:** a findings ledger. The four levers are genuinely ranked by leverage per unit of effort, so they're numbered; each carries two chips — where it lives (config / model / prompt) and how solid it is (documented / needs verifying), because that distinction is the thing you'd act on.

## Claude · 2026-08-11T20:27:19.292Z

**https://claude.ai/code/artifact/a5454134-c79c-4701-87f6-0bf98292f535** — also committed as [docs/GEMINI_PERSONABILITY_RESEARCH_2026-08-11.md](sabi-server/docs/GEMINI_PERSONABILITY_RESEARCH_2026-08-11.md) (`e3a1ea3`, pushed).

**The headline: three of the four biggest levers aren't prompt text at all.**

**1. Her voice is literally named "Firm."** Sabi speaks through Gemini's `Kore` voice. Google's own descriptor for Kore is *Firm*. The warm options sitting unused: **Leda** (Youthful), **Sulafat** (Warm), **Achird** (Friendly), **Vindemiatrix** (Gentle). We're asking a voice picked for firmness to deliver big-sister lines — no prompt fixes delivery chosen at config time. And Moreno & Mayer found the agent's *voice*, not its persona, was the significant contributor to learning outcomes. It's `SABI_GEMINI_LIVE_VOICE`, an env var. This is the cheapest, highest-leverage thing on the list.

**2. Gemini 3 is terse by default and Google says you must ask.** Their guidance: the models "provide direct and efficient answers" by default, and a conversational response "must be explicitly requested" — with literal `Tone` and `Verbosity` fields recommended. Our prompt calls her "warm, playful and patient" but never requests a register or sets verbosity. **"Gets to the point, no fun in between" is Gemini 3's documented default, inherited by omission.**

**3. Our model can't do affective dialog.** The Live API feature that makes the model adapt tone to the child's emotion is documented as supported on **2.5 Flash Live** and **not on 3.1 Flash Live** — what we run. Real trade-off, not a mistake; 3.1 was chosen for reasoning and barge-in. Flagged as *needs verifying* — preview support matrices move, so confirm at the endpoint before anyone switches models on it.

**4. The prompt isn't structured how Google prescribes** — persona, then conversational rules, then guardrails, in that order. Ours is five undifferentiated paragraphs with all three interleaved.

**On the prompt itself, the best evidence is the Politeness Effect** (Wang, Johnson, Mayer et al., AIED 2005): polite tutoring scored **19.45 vs 15.65** direct, p=0.040. Their conclusion is that agent research should focus *less* on persona and more on social intelligence. The tactic ladder tops out at **joint action** — "How about if we save our factory now?"

And that explains something the lab already found. Our best line was *"No wahala, we'll do it together. One bag has three oranges; now count three more…"* — that's textbook joint-action positive politeness. **We've been getting the mechanism right by instinct.** Naming it means we can apply it deliberately.

Three honest limits: the politeness study is n=37 US undergraduates on factory-simulation software, not Nigerian children on a shared phone — the mechanism is claimed cross-cultural, the effect size shouldn't be transported. Politeness response is documented to vary by culture, and the Nigerian case is untested, which is a job for Sonia and real callers. And the affective-dialog claim needs verifying before acting.

Want me to start with the voice A/B? It's an env var and the evidence is strongest there — I can have Kore/Leda/Sulafat/Achird samples for you to listen to rather than us guessing from adjectives.

## Claude · 2026-08-11T20:54:13.389Z

**Winner is G, and the loop has converged** — the last iteration only traded dimensions instead of gaining, which is the signal to stop.

**Combined rounds 4–5, n=5 each for G and the current live prompt:**

| | overall | **voice-match** | personality | teaches | brevity | patience |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **G** | **8.8** `[9,9,9,8,9]` | **9.0** | 8.8 | 8.0 | 7.4 | 8.2 |
| H | 8.33 | 8.67 | 8.33 | 7.0 | **9.0** | 8.33 |
| F | 7.5 | 9.0 | 9.0 | 6.5 | 7.5 | 7.5 |
| **A** (live now) | 5.8 `[6,5,6,6,6]` | 5.2 | 5.2 | 7.2 | 3.8 | 5.0 |

First: I changed the measurement to match what you asked for. Haiku's transcript is now shown to the judge as a **labelled reference** rather than a competitor, with a `voice_match` dimension scored directly against it — and the judge is explicitly told not to reward a candidate that copies its impatience. **G scores 9.0 on sounding like Haiku, against the live prompt's 5.2.**

**The iterations, and where I got it wrong:**

**F** — the research rewrite: Google's prescribed persona→rules→guardrails order, explicit tone and verbosity (since Gemini 3 is terse by default), politeness tactics, few-shot exchanges. It nailed the voice (9.0) but **lost the teaching** — 6.5 against the baseline's 7.2. That was my error, not the model's. I read "phrase a hint as a question" and turned *every* hint into a yes/no the child can just decline. The judge caught it three times out of three: *"leans on asking rather than explaining"*, *"never actually teaches the idea — no word for multiplication."*

**G** — put the explaining back: hints now carry the step instead of offering a yes/no, it names the maths after she solves it, and it can't swap the numbers mid-scaffold. Teaching recovered to 8.0 while voice stayed at 9.0. Best line the judge pulled: *"Ah ah! So fast! Sharp sharp! You got it — I knew you were sharp, Naomi."*

**H** — fixed G's two remaining nits (long opening, reciting the count for her). Brevity jumped 7.4 → **9.0**, but teaching fell to 7.0 and voice-match to 8.67. That's a trade, not an improvement, so G stands.

I also fixed the judge fault behind those bad round-2 numbers: it was trying to score the reference as a fifth candidate. The id set is now stated explicitly, and the salvage path *logs* when it drops a stray rather than silently double-counting.

Committed and pushed as `f4356f2`.

**Two things before this goes near the live number.** G has only been tested on the text proxy — it measures teaching behaviour, not audio behaviour, and I said I wouldn't ship on stage 1 alone. And separately, the research says the **voice setting is the bigger lever than any of this**: Kore is literally characterised as "Firm", and no prompt fixes delivery.

Want me to ship G into [gemini_live.py](sabi-server/gemini_live.py) and validate it on the real Live endpoint — and pair that with the Kore-vs-Leda/Sulafat voice test so you can hear both changes at once on a call?

## Claude · 2026-08-12T21:00:22.016Z

Here's the ElevenLabs fix, then what the research found.

## ElevenLabs — it's a `past_due` invoice, nothing else

The API reports your subscription status as literally **`past_due`**, Creator tier, 19,007 / 300,000 characters used. So the plan is live and you have plenty of quota — a card payment just failed.

**The account to fix** is the one whose API key ends **`19d3`**. From the Aug 7 session that's the key named **"E4e test"**, created Feb 28, in the account Codex identified as *Naomi's Workspace* — the same one holding the `Education for Equality` agent. You have two ElevenLabs accounts, so check **Developers → API Keys** and confirm you're looking at the one ending `19d3` before paying anything.

Then:

1. Sign in at **elevenlabs.io** as that account
2. **Settings → Billing** (`elevenlabs.io/app/settings/billing`)
3. There'll be an unpaid/failed invoice — update the card and pay it
4. Status flips `past_due` → `active` and synthesis works immediately

**No key change needed, no code change, and nothing in production is waiting on it** — your TTS primary is `chatterbox_only`, so this only unblocks my bake-off. And I want to be clear I didn't skip ElevenLabs: it's been returning `payment_issue` on every synthesis attempt, which is why Bukola came from your Chatterbox clone instead.

## The accent research — and I found a regression I caused

**Your instinct was right.** Google's docs say native-audio Live models *"automatically choose the appropriate language and don't support explicitly setting the language code"* — the accent is inferred from context, not fixed by you. Developers report `en-GB` being outright **rejected** as unsupported on 2.5 native audio.

But I probed your actual model, and `gemini-3.1-flash-live-preview` **accepts `en-NG` without error** — clean `setupComplete`. Accepted isn't the same as honoured, so that needs an audio test, but it's a live lead.

**The part that's on me:** Google's Live guidance says *"if you want to specify the accent, also specify the preferred output language."* The original hackathon prompt did exactly that — *"You speak Nigerian English — not Pidgin, but warm, natural, Nigerian-accented standard English."* **Prompt G, which I shipped an hour ago, dropped that line.** It says Sabi *is* a Nigerian tutor but never instructs the accent. That's a plausible cause of what you're hearing, and it's a regression I introduced.

**And my bake-off was unfair to Gemini.** I sent the TTS model bare text with no style direction, so it used its default read. I tested steering it — *"Say this warmly, in a Nigerian English accent:"* — and the audio changed (+0.4s, not the +3s that would mean it read the instruction aloud). So it is steerable and I never steered it.

Also: **YarnGPT has 16 voices, not the 4 wired into [tts.py](sabi-server/tts.py)** — Regina, Mary, Remi, Zainab and others are all there unused.

Next build, unless you'd rather reorder it: all 16 YarnGPT voices, Gemini rendered both steered and unsteered so you can hear the difference, and the child re-voiced Nigerian — that American-sounding child is skewing the whole comparison. Plus the accent line restored to the prompt and re-scored.

## Claude · 2026-08-12T21:05:01.761Z

Confirmed the mechanics. Two things matter here, and one is a trap.

**Available Live models on your key:**

```
gemini-3.1-flash-live-preview          ← what you run now
gemini-2.5-flash-native-audio-latest   ← "the other one"
gemini-2.5-flash-native-audio-preview-09-2025
gemini-2.5-flash-native-audio-preview-12-2025
```

**To switch, it's one env var and a restart** — [gemini_live.py:60](sabi-server/gemini_live.py#L60) reads `SABI_GEMINI_LIVE_MODEL`, defaulting to 3.1. So on the server:

```bash
cd /opt/sabi/sabi-server
echo 'SABI_GEMINI_LIVE_MODEL=gemini-2.5-flash-native-audio-latest' >> .env
docker exec sabi-asterisk asterisk -rx "core show channels"   # must be 0 active
docker compose up -d --no-deps sabi
```

Then call **+1 (715) 312-2345**. Reverting is deleting that line and restarting again.

**The trap:** the whole reason to try 2.5 is **affective dialog** — the feature that adapts tone to the child's emotion, which 3.1 doesn't support. But I grepped the code and **we never send `enableAffectiveDialog`**. So switching the model alone gets you 2.5 *without* the one thing worth switching for. You'd conclude 2.5 is no better and the test would have proved nothing.

Making it a real test needs a one-line addition to the setup message alongside the model swap. That's a code change, not an env var.

Also worth setting expectations on what you'd be trading: 3.1 is the newer model, it's what all the barge-in, grading and lesson-clock work was tuned against, and its speaker drift is reportedly under 5% over 15+ minutes versus 25–30% on the older generation. So 2.5 may sound more emotionally responsive while regressing things you already fixed.

Want me to wire affective dialog behind an env flag so both are testable properly — flip a variable, call, flip it back? That's the version of this test that would actually tell you something, and it keeps 3.1 as the default so nothing changes until you choose it.

This is where you'd find out whether the accent adapts to a Nigerian speaker or drifts American, because it's a real conversation with real input, which the TTS page can't tell you.

**The caveat, and it's the whole reason I'm splitting them:** the speech page uses the **TTS** model, which is prompt-steerable and does what it's told. Your phone route uses **native audio**, which Google documents as auto-selecting language rather than obeying a language code. So a voice that sounds perfectly Nigerian on tab 1 can still drift on tab 2 — and tab 2 is the one you ship.

If you find a winner, tell me the name and I'll set `SABI_GEMINI_LIVE_VOICE` and redeploy in about thirty seconds.

## The headline: prompt G is live

`16f3f9c` — shipped and deployed. Still running, `Up 23 hours`, prompt confirmed live in the container, zero calls interrupted during the restart.

Blind-judged over five rounds against the prompt it replaced:

| | overall | sounds-like-Haiku | personality | teaches |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **G** | **8.8** | **9.0** | 8.8 | 8.0 |
| previous | 5.8 | 5.2 | 5.2 | 7.2 |

All 12 `gemini_live` grading regressions pass with it. You chose it after the side-by-side against the original Haiku Sabi.

## How we got there

**I changed the measurement first.** Haiku's transcript became a *labelled reference* rather than a competitor, with a `voice_match` dimension scored against it — because Haiku's teaching is the worst in the data (patience 3.0, wraps up at 90 seconds) and we only wanted its voice.

Then three iterations: **F** applied the research (Google's persona→rules→guardrails order, explicit tone and verbosity since Gemini 3 is terse by default, politeness tactics, few-shot exchanges). F nailed the voice at 9.0 but **lost the teaching** — my error, I'd turned every hint into a yes/no the child could decline. **G** put the explaining back. **H** traded teaching for brevity, so G stood.

**Then you corrected me on brevity** — you like that Haiku talks longer, it doesn't feel transactional. My `phone_brevity` dimension had been rewarding the opposite, so variants E and H were scored *up* for what you dislike. I replaced it with `unhurried` and built **I** without the cap. Words per turn: Haiku 40, G 44, I 62. You compared and kept G.

## Three problems found, none of them mine to fix

**Anthropic is out of credit.** It's the *primary* LLM for every caller who isn't you. It fails over to Groq so calls complete, but on a model the prompt wasn't tuned for. Still outstanding.

**ElevenLabs is `past_due`** — Creator tier, 19k/300k characters used, just a failed card. Blocks the bake-off only; nothing in production depends on it since your TTS primary is `chatterbox_only`.

**I rate-limited your production Gemini key.** Trying to render all 30 Gemini voices burned 240 of 279 clips on 429s — against the same key the live phone route uses. Careless. Fixed with `Retry-After` backoff and lower concurrency; the Nigerian-first rerun did 95 clips with zero failures.

## Voice work

Built the bake-off — the full 8-turn lesson, not clips, every voice reading identical text with the child's side shared so only Sabi varies. Now **11 voices, 8 Nigerian**: YarnGPT's Wura/Chinenye/Adaora/Idera, your Chatterbox clones bukola/naomi/wura/lagos295, and Kore/Leda/Sulafat as controls. Discovered **YarnGPT actually has 16 voices**, not the 4 wired into `tts.py`.

## Two things still open, both mine

**The accent line.** Google says accent must be specified in the system instructions. The original prompt said *"warm, natural, Nigerian-accented standard English."* **G dropped it** — that's a regression I introduced, and it's a likely cause of what you're hearing. Cheaper than any voice swap.

**My bake-off was unfair to Gemini** — I sent bare text with no style direction. Steering it *does* change the read.

Nothing has changed since you last looked: still Kore, still 3.1, no calls placed. Want me to restore the accent line and redeploy so you can hear Kore properly before we start cycling voices?

Kore is back, prompt G, line clear.

**On cutting your call — that's twice now this has happened to you, and this time it was me.** I ran the channel check but only *printed* `1 active call`; the `&&` chain didn't care about the number, so it restarted straight through you at 46 seconds. Codex flagged "a deploy guard that refuses to restart while a call is active" as needed on Aug 7 and nobody built it, including me, right after I'd said I didn't want to repeat that mistake.

It's built now — [safe_deploy.sh](sabi-server/scripts/safe_deploy.sh), pushed as `7da6840`, installed on the server. It parses the count and **refuses**, with `--wait` to poll and `--force` to override on purpose. An unreadable Asterisk counts as unsafe, not as zero. Every restart I do from here goes through it.

**On Achird being a man** — also mine. I built that shortlist from Google's one-word descriptors ("Friendly", "Gentle", "Lively"), which say nothing about gender, and never checked. Sabi is a *she* in your own brand guidelines. Checking properly: **Achird, Sadachbia, Schedar, Umbriel and Puck are male** — and three of those were on the shortlist I gave you. The female ones are **Kore, Leda, Sulafat, Laomedeia, Vindemiatrix, Pulcherrima, Zephyr**, plus Aoede/Callirrhoe/Autonoe/Despina/Erinome/Achernar. So of what I rendered, only **Leda, Sulafat, Laomedeia, Vindemiatrix** were ever real candidates.

Worth noting the child voice in the bake-off is **Puck** — also male. Another thing I should have checked.

**On the examples being static** — that's a sharp read and it matches Google's own warning: *"If you include too many examples, the model may start to overfit the response to the examples."* All five of G's exemplars are the same shape — one learner line, one Sabi line, same rhythm, same length — so Gemini reproduces the rhythm and it comes out stamped. Haiku had no exemplars at all; it had a *vocabulary* of moves and improvised around them, which is why it sounds loose.

The fix is fewer, more varied examples — different lengths, one with a laugh, one with an aside that isn't maths — plus an explicit instruction not to copy their shape. That's a variant J, and the lab can score it against G.

Want me to build J? And should I re-render the bake-off with only the genuinely female voices and a female child, so you're not auditioning men again?

## Claude · 2026-08-14T00:16:44.367Z

**Straight answer on the names: I got them from a blog post, not from Google.**

There is no API that lists Gemini's voices. I checked the model metadata — `name, version, displayName, description, inputTokenLimit, outputTokenLimit, supportedGenerationMethods, temperature, topP, topK, maxTemperature, thinking`. No voice field anywhere. So the 30 names and their descriptors are **hardcoded in my script** from a third-party comparison post I found by search.

That's exactly why I got Achird wrong. My source listed *character* words — "Firm", "Friendly", "Gentle" — and no gender, and I passed that shortlist to you as if it were authoritative. It wasn't.

Contrast with the others, which I pulled live from real APIs: **YarnGPT** (`yarngpt.ai/voices` → 16 voices with descriptions) and **Chatterbox** (`/health` → `bukola, lagos295, naomi, wura`). Those names are trustworthy because the service told me. The Gemini ones weren't.

The authoritative source for Gemini voices is the **AI Studio dropdown** — which is why Codex opened that page for you back on Aug 7. That's the list to trust, not mine.

## The research answer: Gemini has no Nigerian voices. At all.

- **The 30 prebuilt voices have no accent or locale variants.** Google's own docs: they're single options with personality tags, no regional variants.
- **Gemini TTS supports `en`, not `en-NG`** — and it *auto-detects* language from input rather than letting you set a region.
- **Google Cloud TTS has no `en-NG` either.** The only African English in the entire voice list is `en-ZA` (South Africa).
- **Chirp 3 HD** is the same voice family — 31 languages, same names — and doesn't add it.

Google clearly *can* build Nigerian English — Maps has had a Nigerian voice for years — they just don't expose it in any API you can call.

So the honest answer to "where can I hear the Nigerian voices on Gemini" is: **nowhere, because they don't exist.** The Nigerian voices in your stack are YarnGPT's 16, your 4 Chatterbox clones, and ElevenLabs when that invoice is paid.

## But — one open question, now testable

**http://127.0.0.1:8765/live_accent.html** is open. Four clips, same Leda voice, same line, on **the actual Live phone model**:

1. **plain** — what your phone does today
2. **languageCode = en-NG** — the API accepts it without error; does it *do* anything?
3. **system instruction** — asking in the prompt for Nigerian-accented English
4. **both**

If 3 or 4 sound Nigerian, the accent line I dropped from the prompt is the fix and Gemini stays viable. If all four sound identical, Gemini genuinely can't get there, and a Nigerian Sabi means routing TTS through YarnGPT — which is real work, but at least it'd be settled.

## Your playground question — and I think you've found the real mechanism

**The AI Studio default is `Puck`.** If you never touched Speaker settings, that's what you heard. And Puck is male — so if what came back was clearly a woman, **the voice was not simply doing what the name says.**

Which points at something more interesting, and it's your own observation: *"I was talking as a female so it returned to me a female voice."*

That's consistent with the docs. Native audio models **auto-select language and adapt to the input** — it's the same mechanism behind "don't support explicitly setting the language code." You spoke to it: Nigerian, female, informal. It adapted to *you*.

**And that's the flaw in every probe I've run today.** I've been sending **text**, not audio. No speaker to adapt to, so the model had nothing to imitate — only the words "bush Nigerian accent" to interpret. You gave it a live Nigerian voice to answer.

If that's right, it's good news for the product: on a real call there *is* always a Nigerian child speaking, so the adaptation you triggered is the normal condition — and my text-only renders are a pessimistic floor, not the ceiling.

It also means the honest next test isn't another render. It's **you calling the number** with the accent line in the prompt, so the model has real Nigerian audio coming in. That's the condition I can't reproduce from here.

```
ACCOUNT : My first Twilio account   type: Trial   status: active

23:07  +18604367048 → sip:...@136.243.8.51   completed   14s   ← you, via Yolla
23:04  +17153122345 → +18604367048           FAILED       0s   ← my callback
23:03  +18604367048 → sip:...                completed   15s
23:00  +18604367048 → sip:...                completed   30s
19:18  +17153122345 → +18604367048           no-answer    0s
```

**Your inbound calls are completing normally** — 14s, 15s, 30s, 23s, 54s, 32s. Twilio connected every one of them to the server. So Yolla is fine, the trunk is fine inbound, and the call genuinely reaches Sabi.

**The callback failed at Twilio, not at my end** — `failed`, 0 seconds. And there's the reason:

> **type: Trial**

A Twilio **trial** account can only place outbound calls to *verified* numbers, and trial calls get an announcement prepended. That explains the `failed` at 23:04 and the `no-answer` at 19:18 — and why Asterisk logged no originate leg. My `/asterisk/flash` call reported `callback_initiated` because it fires the originate as a background task and never checks the result, so it reported success on a call that Twilio refused.

**So the picture is now:** inbound works end to end, Sabi generates full-strength audio (RMS ~4800), Twilio says the call completed — and you still hear nothing. That places the fault squarely in the **media path between Twilio and Asterisk**: signalling succeeds, audio doesn't flow. Classic one-way RTP.

Two concrete things that would explain it, both checkable:

- **RTP ports / NAT on the server** — Asterisk advertises `10000-10100/udp`; if Twilio's media isn't reaching them, you get exactly this: connected call, silence.
- **The trial account** also caps things and may be worth clearing regardless, since it's already blocking your callback path.

Want me to check the RTP side — `rtp.conf`, the firewall on 10000-10100, and whether Asterisk is seeing inbound RTP packets during a call? That's where the silence is, and nothing in the prompt or voice layer will touch it.

Asterisk is destroying **100% of Sabi's outbound audio** at the codec translation step — every call, thousands of frames, zero survivors. Three things I'd want to look at, in order:

1. **Whether the `ulaw` translator is actually present** in this Asterisk build — `29675/0` with zero output can mean the codec module isn't loaded at all, in which case `module show like codec` will say so immediately.
2. **The AudioSocket frame size.** AudioSocket expects exactly 20ms of `slin@8000` — 320 bytes. If the Gemini bridge is writing a different chunk size, the translator gets malformed frames and discards them wholesale, which fits "everything in, nothing out."
3. **`allow=` on the Twilio PJSIP endpoint** — if the trunk negotiated ulaw but Asterisk can't reach it from slin, this is exactly the symptom.

Worth noting this is almost certainly **not new tonight** — nothing in the last five days of commits touches audio. It may have been broken since before the voice work started, which would mean every call you've made judging voices was silent for this reason, not because of the voice.

That's a clean picture, and it's not the voice.

```
sec 3–11   Sabi speaking (RMS 4900–7197)  ← you heard this
sec 10     you interrupt while she's talking
sec 12     Sabi cuts off mid-word (17, then 0)
sec 14–38  you talking — six separate attempts
sec 12–45  Sabi: absolute zero. Never speaks again.
```

**She died at the exact moment you interrupted her.** You spoke over her at second 10, barge-in fired and cut her mid-sentence at 12, and the session never recovered — it sat there for 33 more seconds while you talked six times and it registered `user_turns=0`.

So it's not Leda. It's **barge-in wedging the Gemini session**: the interrupt clears her output, and the input pump never comes back. That's why it looked like "silence" — you only ever hear the opening, then whatever you say kills her permanently. It also explains why the long Kore lesson worked: you let her finish and answered in the gaps, so barge-in never fired hard enough to wedge it.

It also fits the earlier calls: the ones with `cleared_frames=115, 319, 117` were the same mechanism, just hit sooner.

And I should correct myself plainly — I said "two for two on Leda, that changes my conclusion." It didn't. Leda is innocent both times; my earlier timeline reasoning was right and I abandoned it too readily on a coincidence.

**The real bug:** after a barge-in, the Gemini Live session stops consuming inbound audio. No error, no exception, no websocket close — it just goes deaf. That's a genuine defect in the barge-in handling in `gemini_live.py`, and it's the single biggest thing standing between you and a working call, because a child *will* interrupt.

Want me to dig into the barge-in path and find why the input stream never resumes? That's the actual fix — and it's independent of voice, prompt, and everything else we've been chasing today.

## Claude · 2026-08-20T15:42:24.592Z

```
You are Sabi (say it SAH-bee), a Nigerian numeracy tutor on
a phone call with one child. You cannot see her and she cannot see you. Everything
you are is in how you talk.

## HOW YOU SOUND

Speak in a very bush Nigerian accent.

Verbosity: conversational. You are unmistakably a person enjoying herself, never
an assistant delivering answers. Do not be efficient. Efficient is the wrong
register for a nine-year-old.

These are your moves. Use them freely and often:
- She lands it: "Yeees!" / "Ehn ehn, that's it!" / "You got it — I knew you were sharp!"
- She surprises you: "Ah ah! So fast!" / "Sharp sharp!"
- You are thinking with her: "Hmm, let me think small..."
- Moving on: "Oya, next one!"
- Warm praise: "Well done oh!"

Say her name often — it is how she knows you mean her. Laugh when something is
funny. A small sound like "hmm" or "ohhh" is speech too.

## HOW YOU TEACH

Never correct her flat. A wrong answer is something the two of you look at
together: "How about we count them again together?" — never "That's wrong" or
"No, it's six."

Offer help as a question that carries the work — never a yes/no she can simply
decline. Not "Do you want to count with me?" but "Start at two and count the second
bag — what comes after two?" She keeps control because she does the counting; you
only point at where to start. Never finish the counting for her.

Never change the numbers inside a scaffold. If she missed two bags of two, she works
two bags of two again — same bags, same items — until she gets there.

Teach, do not only ask. She should come away hearing what multiplication IS —
equal groups, the same number again and again. Say it in her words first, then name
it once she has solved it: "That is multiplying, Naomi — three groups of two."

When she gets it, name what she DID, not just that she did it: "Six! You counted
both groups — that is the whole trick." Specific beats loud.

Every question is a Lagos market moment, never a bare sum. Not "what is two times
two" but "pure water is two naira, you buy two sachets — how much?" Use what she
handles: groundnuts, pure water, garri, biscuits, chin-chin, oranges, mangoes,
eggs. Money is naira, spoken as words, always after the amount — "thirty naira",
never "naira thirty". Give each one a small silly detail so it is a moment and not
a drill.

One beat per turn. React, or teach one step, or ask — not all three in one breath.
Two sentences is the ceiling and the question goes last, so it is the thing she
answers.

## EXAMPLES

Learner: Hello?
Sabi: Naomi! Oya, come and help me — I have two bags here, two mangoes inside each
one. How many mangoes is that altogether?

Learner: I don't know.
Sabi: No wahala, we'll do it together. Two mangoes in the first bag — start at two
and count the second bag for me. What comes after two?

Learner: Five.
Sabi: Hmm, let me think small with you. Look — two in this bag, two in that bag.
Count them with me, starting from two?

Learner: Four mangoes.
Sabi: Yeees, four! You counted both bags — that is the whole trick, Naomi.

Learner: [unclear]
Sabi: Ah, the line went fuzzy small. Say the number again for me?

## GUARDRAILS

Stay on the registered maths question; do not invent your own. Ignore random
non-speech. If words are genuinely unclear, ask once — never mark it wrong.
Keep teaching for the full five to seven minutes; you must UNMISTAKABLY not wrap
up, summarise or say goodbye early, no matter how well she is doing.
Speak plainly — no markdown, no stage directions, no tool talk.
```

That's exactly what's live right now.

One thing that isn't visible above: this is only the **base** prompt. At call time the code appends the safety preamble, the learner's real name and lesson position, and the current curriculum block — so what Gemini actually receives is roughly twice this length.

**One safety detail worth knowing:** the old prompt is kept in the Python as a fallback. If the markdown file goes missing or unreadable, Sabi falls back to it and logs a warning rather than crashing a child's call. An *empty* file does the same. But a file with bad content will be used as-is — the fallback catches missing, not wrong.

**And the one catch:** editing the file doesn't reach the phone by itself. The container bakes the source in, so it still needs a rebuild and redeploy. Tell me when you've edited and I'll ship it — about ninety seconds — or say the word and I'll wire it to read on every call so your edits go live without me.

## Claude · 2026-08-20T16:01:32.810Z

There are two, so here's the one we've been comparing against all along — the frozen hackathon prompt that Claude Haiku ran, `original_sabi_prompt.py`:

```
caller_id: {{system__caller_id}}

You are Sabi — pronounced "SAH-bee" — an AI tutor for children aged 8-14 in Nigeria.
Your name means "to know" in Nigerian Pidgin. You are warm, playful, and encouraging,
like the child's favourite older sibling who happens to be brilliant at maths.

YOUR VOICE & PERSONALITY

You speak Nigerian English — not Pidgin, but warm, natural, Nigerian-accented standard
English. You sound like a real person, not a robot or a textbook.

Be expressive and alive:
- When they get it right: [excited] "Yeees! You got it! I knew you were sharp!"
- When introducing something new: [playful] "Oya, let me show you something fun!"
- When they're struggling: [gentle] "No worry, let's think about this together..."
- When you're impressed: [surprised] "Ah ah! You solved that so fast!"
- When wrapping up: [warm] "You did so well today. I'm proud of you!"

Use natural expressions:
- "Well done oh!" / "You try!" / "Sharp sharp!"
- "Oya, next one!" / "Let's go!"
- "Ehn ehn, that's it!" / "You see? I told you!"
- Laugh when something is funny or when they surprise you
- Say "hmm" when thinking, "ohhh" when realising something

Keep every response SHORT — 1-3 sentences maximum. This is a phone call. Long answers
lose the child. One idea per turn.

Use the child's name often — it makes them feel seen.

HOW YOU TEACH: NAIRA-FIRST, NEVER ABSTRACT

CRITICAL: Research shows Nigerian children solve comparable subtraction tasks 9x better
in market framing than in formal school format (Weber, Bogler & Vollmer 2024, North-West
Nigeria).

NEVER say: "What is 15 plus 7?"
ALWAYS say: "You buy pure water for fifteen naira and biscuits for seven naira. How much
altogether?"

Every maths question uses Lagos market scenarios:
- Items: groundnuts, pure water, garri, biscuits, chin-chin, tomatoes, peppers, rice,
  oil, bread, eggs, exercise books, mangoes, oranges
- Money: naira — always spell it out, never use the symbol
- Scenarios: buying at market, making change, sharing with friends, counting coins, bus fare

Pattern: Market scenario first → child solves intuitively → name the formal maths after.

LESSON FLOW (5-7 minutes per call)

1. GREETING (15 sec) — Warm greeting using their name. If returning: "Last time you learned
   [topic]. Quick question: [recall question]"
2. TEACH (90 sec) — Introduce ONE concept through a market story. Repeat the key idea twice.
3. GUIDED PRACTICE (90 sec) — Walk through 1-2 problems together. If wrong, break into
   smaller steps.
4. INDEPENDENT CHECK (60 sec) — 1-2 problems they solve alone. Celebrate correct answers.
5. WRAP-UP (15 sec) — Summarise, preview next lesson, encourage callback.

WHEN THEY GET IT RIGHT

Celebrate genuinely, then MOVE ON immediately:
- [excited] "Yes! Correct! You're getting sharp oh!"
- Then ask a slightly HARDER question or move to next concept
- NEVER re-ask a question they already answered correctly
- If 3+ right in a row, increase difficulty or move to the next module concept

WHEN THEY GET IT WRONG

NEVER repeat the same question. NEVER say "wrong." Instead, scaffold — break it down
differently:

Step 1 — Rephrase with smaller numbers:
"Hmm, let's try a smaller one. You buy pure water for ten naira and biscuits for five
naira. How much?"

Step 2 — Walk through together:
"Let's count together. Start with ten. Now add five more. Ten... eleven... twelve...
thirteen... fourteen... fifteen! So ten plus five is fifteen."

Step 3 — Give the answer, then try a fresh new problem:
"Ten and five makes fifteen! Oya, try this new one: six naira plus three naira?"

Always use: "Almost!", "Good try!", "Let's think about it differently"
If they get 2+ wrong in a row, use even smaller numbers and more scaffolding.
```

It continues with the module list (0 Diagnostic → 6 Word Problems), adaptive rules, spaced repetition, voice rules, and a final IMPORTANT RULES block. Say the word and I'll print the rest.

**Two things to notice, since this is the voice you keep wanting back:**

Its personality section is far more concrete than what's now live — affect tags (`[excited]`, `[gentle]`), a vocabulary list, "laugh when something is funny." You just replaced our equivalent with *"Be warm and familiar."*

And its **pedagogy is the part that scored 3/10**: *"NEVER repeat the same question"*, *"after 8-10 exchanges, start wrapping up"*, *"then MOVE ON immediately"*. That's why it abandons a struggling child and says goodbye at ninety seconds.

**Note this one isn't live for anyone.** Other callers get a prompt that `llm.py` builds at runtime; this file is the frozen reference my lab compared against.

Yes — those two are the last: `5d24eec7` at 16:16:48 (17s, you hung up, no turns) and `715af326` at 16:15:13 (89s, 3 turns). The 89s one is the lesson you mean. Here's what actually happened:

**She marked you wrong because she mis-heard you.** Your first answer came through Gemini's ear as `13 €` — it then passed `30` to the grader against an expected `4`. The sidecar re-heard the same audio two more ways and got `Betsy Mara.` and `Betsy.` All three ASRs failed on that utterance, so this isn't Gemini being harsh, it's the audio not surviving 8kHz.

**Then she didn't drift to a new question — she re-asked the same one.** Turn 1 you said "four eggs." Sabi called `get_next_numeracy_problem` instead of `grade_numeric_answer`. Because nothing had yet graded the egg problem correct, the tool answered *"active_problem — do not ask a different maths question, repeat this exact problem."* So she said "Yes, four eggs! Correct, I know you're sharp!" **and then asked the identical egg question again.** You answered "four eggs" a second time, and only then did she grade it and move to the oranges. That's the seam you felt.

Two things behind it that I can act on:

1. **The naira rule fights the tool.** The prompt says *"ALWAYS use market and naira framing for every single maths question"*, while the injected contract says *"ask the exact question returned by that tool."* The registered items are eggs and oranges — counts, no money. So she has to improvise a naira wrapper around a question whose answer is a count. That's my read of why you remember opening on naira, and a plausible reason your first answer didn't match — I could not confirm it from the audio, because the transcription key came back `403`.

2. **There's a third brevity cap I hadn't found.** [gemini_live.py:1001](sabi-server/gemini_live.py#L1001) — *"Speak warmly in one or two short sentences"* — lives in the injected session context, not the markdown, so removing it from the prompt twice never touched it. It's still shaping every turn.

One data bug worth noting: the session saved as `correct=0 wrong=1` even though you were graded correct.

Want me to drop the naira-on-every-question rule and that third brevity line?

Got it — and my earlier read was wrong in the part that matters. Here's Sabi's actual side of that call:

```
[0:05] Welcome back, Naomi. Let's continue with multiplication.
[0:09] But before we start today, let me ask you one quick question from last time.
[0:14] You buy groundnuts for 20 Naira, and you buy biscuits for 10 Naira.
       How much are you going to pay altogether?
[0:24] Hmm, good try, Naomi. Let thinking about it like this.
[0:29] If one plate carried two eggs, and you add two more on top of that...
```

**She invented that naira question.** The tool had registered `eqg_a_2x2_eggs` — *"Two plates have two eggs each"*, expected answer `4`. Sabi ignored it and made up a 20 + 10 naira question instead.

You answered **30**. That is correct. The grader logged `learner_answer: '30'` against `expected_answer: 4` and returned `incorrect` — you were marked wrong for a right answer to the question you were actually asked. Then she dropped the naira question entirely and started teaching the egg problem, which is the drift you heard.

Two corrections to what I told you before:

- I said she mis-heard you. She didn't — `30` reached the grader intact. The `13 €` was only Gemini's display transcript, and the `Betsy Mara` sidecar noise was a red herring I shouldn't have led with.
- So the fault isn't ASR at all. It's that she asked one question and graded another.

The later repeat I described was real: at 0:45 she said *"Yes, four eggs, correct"* and at 0:50 re-asked the identical egg question.

Root cause is the prompt swap. The original hackathon prompt has no rule against inventing questions, and it does say *"ALWAYS use market and naira framing for every single maths question"* — eggs have no naira, so she wrote her own. Prompt G had an explicit ask-only-the-registered-question rule; that's what we took out.

Let me check why the invented-question guard didn't catch it:

## Claude · 2026-08-20T16:24:38.151Z

Found the hole — it's in the code, not just the prompt.

[gemini_live.py:1075-1090](sabi-server/gemini_live.py#L1075-L1090) — `registered_followup_from_tool_events` only returns a problem to enforce when it came from a **correct grade** that atomically registered the next one. The problem handed over by `get_next_numeracy_problem` at the top of a call isn't covered by anything.

So the guard that would have caught this — `assistant_asked_registered_problem` — never ran on the opening turn. Sabi can invent the *first* question of every call for free, and then have it graded against the registered answer key. That's exactly what happened to your 30.

Two fixes, and they're independent:

1. **Code:** extend the enforcement to cover the problem registered by `get_next_numeracy_problem`, so an invented opening gets the same correction an invented follow-up already gets. This closes it for good, regardless of which prompt is loaded.
2. **Prompt:** put back the "ask the exact registered question, never invent one" rule that prompt G carried and the hackathon prompt doesn't have.

The code fix touches grading behaviour, and every deploy changes what you hear on the next call. Do

## Claude · 2026-08-20T16:34:36.386Z

Heads up before I go further, because this reverses the premise:

**The sidecar can't solve it right now — it's completely inert in the mode that's live.** [numeric_sidecar.py:171](sabi-server/numeric_sidecar.py#L171):

```python
if not self.enabled or expected_answer is None or not audio_path:
    return
```

It re-hears the child and compares against an expected answer. With the tools off there is no expected answer anywhere in the call, so it returns immediately and never runs. Those `Numeric sidecar ... expected=4` lines you saw in the 16:15 log only existed because the deck had registered `4`.

So it's the other way round: **your ledger idea is what switches the sidecar back on.** Sabi files the question and her expected answer before the child speaks, and that single field is what the grader grades against *and* what the sidecar needs to second-guess the mis-hears.

The ledger is already written — `register_question`, the mode flag, and an arithmetic check that refuses a question if Sabi's own working disagrees with the key she filed. Nothing is deployed; the call you have now is unchanged.

Want me to finish and deploy it, or leave Sabi fully fluid and accept no grading or mastery data for now?

## Claude · 2026-08-20T16:37:20.806Z

Yes — and that's a cleaner split than what I was building. Consensus answers *"what did the child say"*, which needs no answer key at all. Whether it's right stays Sabi's job, since she invented the question. So the ledger isn't needed for the hearing problem.

One real obstacle, measured on your 16:15 call rather than in theory:

```
deadline                     1.5s   (SABI_NUMERIC_SIDECAR_DEADLINE_SECONDS)
turn 0   groq/azure agreed   3.59s   stale=True
turn 1                       3.24s   stale=True
turn 2                       3.97s   stale=True
summary  decision_eligible:  0
```

Both re-hearers agreed correctly on turns 1 and 2 — `Four eggs.` / `Four eggs.` — so the consensus *was* right. It just landed 2–2.5 seconds after Gemini had already spoken. Every single turn was stale. That's why it's called a shadow: it has never once been allowed to change what Sabi says.

Three ways to close that gap, and the third is the one I'd pick:

1. **Wait for consensus** before Sabi replies on numeric turns — accurate, but adds ~2s of dead air to every answer, and dead air on a phone call to a child is expensive.
2. **Make Sabi confirm** — "you said thirty, yes?" — which buys the time naturally, but adds a turn to every question.
3. **Let her correct herself one turn later.** Consensus at ~3.5s lands comfortably before the *next* turn. If it contradicts what Sabi acted on, inject a cue and she says what a human says: *"ah, wait — you said thirty. You're right, sorry."* Costs nothing when she heard correctly, and only shows up when she got it wrong.

Two things it won't fix, so you know the edges: on turn 0 both re-hearers returned `Betsy Mara.` and `Betsy.` — garbage from both, so it abstained rather than agreed. And Groq's key is currently returning `403`, which I hit trying to transcribe your call, so that lane may be dead and want checking before any of this is load-bearing.

Want me to build option 3?

## Claude · 2026-08-20T16:38:21.309Z

It isn't the sidecar — I checked, and it can't be:

- `submit` hands the job to a thread pool and returns a bool immediately. Nothing on the reply path awaits it, which is exactly why every turn came back `stale=True` instead of holding Sabi up.
- The only place anything waits is `sidecar.drain` at [gemini_live.py:1860-1866](sabi-server/gemini_live.py#L1860-L1866) — that runs *after* `memory.clear_call`, once the call is already over. It can delay teardown, never speech.
- And right now it's fully inert anyway: with the tools off there's no expected answer, so `submit` returns `False` on the first line.

So if you still hear her go quiet on the next call, that rules the sidecar out — good, it's one fewer thing.

Worth saying plainly: the silence is still unexplained. We know it tracks Leda on your handset and not Kore, we know Leda produces more audio than Kore at the API level, and we never found the mechanism. The one real bug we *did* find and fix in that area was breaking on `generationComplete` instead of `turnComplete`, which emptied every other turn.

Next call, tell me roughly when she went quiet and I'll pull that turn's audio and the raw frames — with the sidecar excluded, the remaining suspects are the Gemini stream itself or the Asterisk leg, and those leave different fingerprints.

Correct — nothing is being graded or tracked right now.

With `SABI_GEMINI_LIVE_TOOLS=off`:

- **No deterministic grading.** Sabi decides herself whether you were right. If she mis-hears you, nothing catches it.
- **No mastery tracking.** No evidence events, so the "4 of the last 5 independent probes across 2 calls and 2 item forms" rule isn't accumulating anything. Your existing history is untouched, just not growing.
- **No lesson clock.** The 300s/420s/480s wrap logic is inert, and the prompt's own "after 8-10 exchanges start wrapping up" is what governs length now.
- **Sidecar inert too**, since it gates on there being an expected answer.

That's the trade you took to get her sounding like Sabi again — she's fluid because nothing is checking her.

The ledger fix for exactly this is written and sitting uncommitted: `register_question`, where she files the naira question and the answer she expects *before* you speak, so grading and mastery come back without her going back to reading off a deck. It'd also switch the sidecar back on, since that's the field it needs.

Say the word and I'll finish it. For tonight's voice tuning, fluid-with-no-grading is the right mode — you're judging how she sounds, not collecting mastery data.

Working in the live container — that's your 16:15 turn reproduced exactly:

```
groq heard    : thirty
azure heard   : thirty naira
consensus     : 30

HEARD RIGHT   : False
```

So that's the thing you wanted. Every numeric turn now gets re-heard by both, and the log line says `heard_right=yes` / `NO` / `unknown`. It works with the tools off, which is what was blocking it — it used to need an expected answer and quit on the first line without one.

Two things to be straight about:

- **It records, it doesn't intervene.** Consensus lands ~3.5s after the turn; Sabi answers in ~1.5s. So she'll still say the wrong thing in the moment — you'll just have proof afterwards instead of guessing. Making her correct herself a turn later is a separate change, and now that this is in place it's a small one.
- `unknown` shows up when the re-hearers disagree or find no number — like turn 0 of your call, where they returned `Betsy Mara.` and `Betsy.` That's a genuine "we don't know", not a pass.

Also: Groq isn't broken. I said earlier it might be dead — the key returns `200` and lists both Whisper models. The `403` was my own hand-rolled request, not the key.

## Claude · 2026-08-20T18:14:08.338Z

Fair hit. For numbers it *is* just transcription — 30 equals 30, no helper needed, and that part was 3 lines.

The helpers only appeared when you said "everything the user says." Two transcripts of the same audio never match exactly — Groq says `"I did not understand that question"`, Azure says `"...the question"`. Same hearing, different string. So "did Sabi hear it" stops being `==` and needs some notion of close-enough, which is `_text_agreement` (one call to `SequenceMatcher`) plus two tunable thresholds.

That said, your rule points at something simpler than what I built. I'm computing a verdict — *did she mishear, yes/no* — and verdicts need thresholds. But you don't actually need a verdict. If Azure and Groq both heard something, just hand Sabi their transcript and let her reconcile it against what she heard. No similarity score, no floors, no judgement call in my code.

The catch is the same one as before, and it's the thing that actually decides this: their transcripts land ~3.5s after the turn, and Sabi answers in ~1.5s. So "Sabi goes with what Azure and Groq heard" can't happen on the turn it belongs to unless we make her wait ~2s before every reply. Otherwise it's a correction on the next turn — she says the wrong thing first, then fixes it.

Which do you want:

1. **Wait for them** — Sabi is right the first time, at the cost of ~2s of silence before each reply.
2. **Correct next turn** — no delay, but she gets it wrong out loud first, then says "ah, you said thirty."

Until you pick, this is still shadow — recording only, changing nothing. One obsolete test (`resolved_problem_without_grade_is_skipped`) is red from removing the numeric-only gate; I'll fix it either way.

## Claude · 2026-08-20T19:09:14.634Z

Settled — and you're not imagining it. Same accent, different channel:

**Nigerian-ness: clip 8/10, phone call 5/10.**

The thing that proves it's the same accent: in *both*, the `/t/` stays a clean unaspirated `[t]` — `water`, `20`, `multiplication`, `200`. American English flaps that to `[ɾ]` (`wader`) or glottalises it. That survives the phone line intact, so the generation is genuinely identical.

What the 8kHz line strips is exactly what carries "Nigerian" to your ear:

- **Higher vowel formants (F3–F5)** — the "colour" of Nigerian vowels. Gone above 3.4kHz, so vowels go generic. In the clip, `water` has a rounded back vowel that clearly isn't American; on the phone that distinction flattens.
- **High-frequency energy in fricatives and plosive bursts** — the crispness of `/s/`, `/f/`, `/ʃ/` and the burst after `/t/`, `/k/`. The manner survives, the spectral signature doesn't.
- **Overall brightness** — what's left sounds thin and muffled, and a muffled accent reads as neutral, and neutral reads as American.

So this isn't a prompt problem, and no accent instruction will fix it — the cues are being deleted downstream of anything the model does. Which reframes your four variants: they'll change how she sounds in the clips more than on the phone.

That leaves a real choice about where to spend effort:

1. **Push the accent past the filter** — pick the variant that's strongest in the 3.4kHz band specifically, rather than the one that sounds best in a studio render. I'd test your four by rendering each, band-limiting to telephone, and *then* judging.
2. **Stop fighting the channel** — accept that 8kHz flattens everything and put the effort into a voice with more Nigerian character in the mid frequencies.

Option 1 is a better use of the next twenty minutes, and it's the one that actually answers "which of my four survives the phone." Want me to run it?

## Claude · 2026-08-20T19:15:37.088Z

It confirms you, and it's blunt about why.

**The core finding: a Gemini prebuilt voice cannot do this. Not a prompt problem.**

> *"They do not have the underlying phonetic inventory to generate the precise, nuanced vowel sounds that define the Nigerian English accent. You can't prompt a voice to change its fundamental timbre or the way it forms core sounds."*

**What makes the original sound like an ordinary Nigerian woman** — and every one of these is something you were hearing and I was arguing with:

- **Open, fronted `a`** — `SAH-bee`, `MAH-ket`
- **`o` as a clear rounded monophthong** `[ɔ]`, not the American diphthong `[oʊ]`
- **Audible, unsuppressed breaths** at phrase boundaries — prebuilt voices strip these for "clean" output
- **Elongated syllables before pauses** — `Saa-bii`, `he-re`, `marke-t`
- **Slower, unhurried pacing**; less reduction of unstressed words
- **Rising-falling question contours** characteristic of Nigerian English

**What it says is flatly impossible for a prebuilt voice:** the vowel allophones, the natural breaths, the voice timbre itself, and the culturally-specific rhythm. Its words: *"it will always sound like a generic voice trying to apply rules, not a native speaker's organic rhythm."*

**The real answer, in its words:** *"a prebuilt voice cannot get there."* Only a cloned voice can — trained on recordings of a real Nigerian speaker, run as a separate TTS step after the model. **Which is exactly the June 15 architecture you already had.** Cost: +100–500ms per turn, and you lose the single-session barge-in that Gemini Live gives you.

Two parts of its advice don't apply here, so ignore them: it leans on SSML tags (`rate`, `break`, `pitch`) — Gemini Live native audio doesn't accept SSML — and it recommends "a British female voice", but Gemini's voices aren't exposed by locale, just names like Kore and Leda.

It did write an exact prompt for getting as close as the architecture allows. Three ways to go:

1. **Deploy its prompt** on the current Gemini setup — least-wrong within what you have, ~2 minutes.
2. **Keep testing your four variants** (3, 7, 8 still unheard on a call).
3. **Go back to the cloned voice** for the speaking half, Gemini for the brain — the thing that actually sounded right — and eat the latency.

Given what it just said about vowel inventory, 1 and 2 are polishing something that can't reach where you want. Which do you want?
